Man City could face further charges as Premier League confronted with ‘dilemma’

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Some executives are pushing for Man City to be investigated for the seasons since 2018 after they were found guilty of 114 charges of breaches of financial rules

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

An increasing number of English football executives are pushing for the Premier League to investigate Manchester City for the seasons since 2018 or bring further charges, although it is a subject causing significant debate and viewed as a potential “dilemma” for the game.

The Abu Dhabi-owned club were last month found guilty of 114 charges of breaches of financial rules in a case already seen as one of the most controversial in football history, although they are currently in the process of appeal.

Amid a temporary lull in the story as the game waits for both the sanctions and the outcome of that appeal, many directors have been pointing to key details of the independent commission’s final verdict.

Since the document laid out how City’s sponsorship deals were inflated by disguised owner investment, with accompanying graphs, an obvious question being asked is whether that just stopped in 2018, despite commercial income accounted as staying at the same level.

As one source asked, “what changed overnight?”

In its decision, the Independent Commission found that Manchester City and its Abu Dhabi-based sponsors were ‘Related Parties’. If the relationships did not change, then that would form the basis for more charges in subsequent seasons given the club did not report this or have the Fair Market Values of the sponsorships assessed.

The ultimate reason for the initial investigation was a tranche of emails from football leaks that were first published by Der Spiegel in November 2018 and covered the period from 2009 to 2018.

While even those who want scrutiny on that period have concerns about another lengthy investigation, the wait for sanctions – expected by many sources to come this month – has sharpened focus on the period after 2018.

There is at least some possibility that the independent commission recommends titles of 2012, 2014 and 2018 are stripped. While this is currently viewed as unlikely, such an outcome would immediately raise more questions about the era of success that followed 2018.

City built on Pep Guardiola’s initial 2017-18 title winning season with the most intense period of glory the Premier League has seen, winning English football’s first four in a row to make it a total of six leagues in seven years alongside a first ever domestic treble and the 2022-23 treble that brought the Champions League.

Executives feel that questions about this have not been adequately addressed by the Premier League, although the competition’s policy and legal guidance has been to refuse to discuss the case until it is completely concluded.

The Premier League have been contacted for comment by The Independent.

There is nevertheless a sense among executives that commercial deals after 2018 will be viewed as having simply been made subject to the associated party rules, and were already assessed at fair market value.

That line of thinking has been met with some scepticism, given the nature of the guilty verdict.

The counter-argument is that associated party rules would be irrelevant here, since any investigation would be about disguised funding.

City are still strenuously insisting on their innocence, and remain bullish about an appeal.

The prospect of further investigation is also seen as representing a potential “dilemma” for the competition and its clubs. Some feel agitating executives are getting ahead of themselves, and that there should just be patience for the current case to conclude.

A concurrent view is that there aren’t the same clear grounds for another investigation without evidence like the leaked emails, and that it might even be unnecessary if the guilty verdict is upheld and the sanctions are appropriate, given what the final verdict already says.

The latter is taken to mean meaningful sporting sanctions and focus on the hierarchy responsible.

Others feel the idea of another investigation is better saved as a “negotiation tactic”, especially with how abrasive City have already been in their position.

As one source said of the guilty verdict prospectively being upheld, “when you’re seeking to agree on a potentially severe punishment, the Premier League can be shown to have given them a concession and spare them further”.

“It is pressure useful for the end game,” another added.

That could also be acutely relevant to the future of the club if this case does conclude with a forced sale under the Independent Football Regulator, or even a voluntary sale, which certain rival executives are mindful of. Any agreement not to investigate further would ensure that potential buyers are not put off by the possibility of further uncertainty and more sanctions.

Some fear that another investigation would take another half-decade or more, and that the Premier League would suffer further when many are keen to eventually draw a line under the case.

The 10 highest-paid CEOs in America, according to the 2026 Equilar 100

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Elon Musk's $132.3 billion award heads this look at 10 of the 100 CEOs in the 2026 Equilar 100, where median pay hit $39.4 million.

Running a large American company means steering it through market upheaval while keeping its finances growing. The strain of that job commands lavish rewards, and those sums raise hard questions about fairness and about who captures the gains of corporate success. In 2025, the median chief executive among the 100 best-paid at U.S. public companies collected $39.4 million. No earlier year in the long-running analysis produced a higher figure, and the median climbed 35.8% from a year earlier. Anyone who owns shares, draws a paycheck from one of these firms, or wonders where corporate money flows has a stake in how boards arrive at those numbers.

The distance between the corner office and the rest of the workforce stretched at a similar clip. The median ratio of chief executive earnings to those of a typical employee reached 475 to 1 in 2025, up from 348 to 1 the year before, a 36.5% jump. The median worker at these employers took home $101,322, a solid income that looks modest beside packages worth tens of millions of dollars. Stock awards drive the surge in executive totals. Base salaries held flat at $1.3 million, while long-term equity accounts for more than 70% of total compensation in the usual package. The research credits movements in stock value with an outsized effect on what an executive receives. A share-price rally can reshape a leader's haul by a margin no salary adjustment could match.

Equilar has partnered with The New York Times for 19 years to track what chief executives earn, and its 2026 study lists the 100 richest pay deals that boards granted to CEOs at U.S.-based or listed companies. Each company needed more than $1 billion in revenue and had to file a proxy statement for its latest completed fiscal year by April 30, 2026. Total compensation sets the order, and the figures cover awards from 2025. The report includes a table of 10 executives from that roster, showing each one's total package, change in pay, and fiscal 2025 revenue. The slides ahead follow those 10 of the 100 in the order the table presents them.

Elon Musk drew a $132.3 billion pay package from Tesla $TSLA in 2025, and all of it came as a single long-term stock award. The grant hinges on aggressive goals for Tesla's market capitalization and its operations. Nothing in the reported total came from salary, bonus, or perks. The structure turns Tesla's chief into an investor whose payday rides on the same share price outside shareholders watch.

Stock awards across the full list look small beside Musk's grant. The median award rose 44.6% in 2025 to $28.4 million, up from $19.6 million. No other package in the study comes within reach of his total. Even the second-largest deal in the table, at Figma, measures in the hundreds of millions, while Musk's runs to the billions.

Musk's package held none of the cash that pads other executives' pay. Median cash compensation, which covers salaries, bonuses, and perks, rose 3.6% to $6.1 million in 2025. Every dollar of his total depends on how Tesla's shares and operations perform. A median executive on the list draws a fixed salary regardless of where the share price lands. Musk's reward rests on his targets alone.

Musk's award surpasses Tesla's $94.8 billion in fiscal 2025 revenue, the largest sales total among the 10 companies in the table. A single grant that outstrips a full year of sales shows how equity tied to market value can lift pay beyond anything a company's revenue would suggest. Sales figures could not explain a sum of this size. The valuation goals carry that weight.

The table lists no change in pay for Musk, leaving the award without a year-over-year comparison. Readers cannot tell from the study whether 2025 marked a leap or a continuation of his earlier pay. What the grant delivers will track the market value and operating results its targets measure.

Figma chief Dylan Field collected $864.4 million in 2025, a 799% jump that gave him the second-largest package among the 100 executives in the study, behind only Tesla $TSLA's Elon Musk. Field co-founded Figma, a cloud-based design tool, and serves as its chief executive. Musk's award runs into the billions. Among the executives paid in millions, nobody topped Field. His award edged past the one Welltower granted Shankh S. Mitra by a slim margin and sits many times above the study's median.

Figma reported $1,055 million in fiscal 2025 revenue, the smallest total among the 10 companies in the table. Every other company in that group brought in more than $4 billion. The leanest firm in the table yielded one of its two biggest awards. Field's package came close to matching everything Figma sold during the year. Outside Tesla, no company in the table handed its chief a sum so large relative to its own sales.

Across all 100 companies in the study, the median company took in $22.8 billion, a mark that dwarfs Figma's turnover. Broadcom $AVGO and Goldman Sachs $GS, two of the largest businesses in the table, gave their chiefs less than Field received. Warner Bros. Discovery, another company many times Figma's size, awarded David M. Zaslav a fraction of Field's sum. Corporate scale did little to determine which executives drew the richest rewards in 2025.

Field's raise trails the gains of four peers in the table, whose increases climbed into the thousands of percent. None of those four took home as much as he did. Even the steepest of those jumps produced a package far smaller than Field's. Percentage change measures movement from a prior award. It reveals little about where an executive finishes. A steep growth rate alone did not carry any executive in this group past Field's dollar total.

Shankh S. Mitra of Welltower received $821,090,355 in 2025, a package 3,965% larger than his award from the year before. Only two executives in the table, Elon Musk and Dylan Field, earned more. Mitra's total trailed Field's by a narrow margin and topped every package listed after his, including the all-equity award to Kasra Nejatian of Opendoor.

A gain of that scale implies Mitra's previous award amounted to a small slice of his 2025 haul. Two leaders in the group posted steeper percentage increases, Niraj Shah of Wayfair and Hock E. Tan of Broadcom $AVGO, yet neither approached Mitra's dollar total. Mitra pairs the third-largest package among the 10 with the third-steepest climb, and no other executive in the table claims a top-three spot on both measures. Field, his nearest rival in dollars, logged a slower rise, and Shah, the fastest riser, finished far behind Mitra in total pay.

Welltower reported $10.8 billion in fiscal 2025 revenue, less than half the $22.8 billion median across the full roster of 100 companies. Five companies in the table brought in more: Tesla $TSLA, Broadcom, Goldman Sachs $GS, Warner Bros. Discovery, and Wayfair. Every one of their chief executives except Musk took home less than Mitra. Welltower outsold Figma, Opendoor, Rivian $RIVN, and Palo Alto Networks $PANW, yet Figma still paid Field more than Welltower paid Mitra.

Nothing in the table explains how Welltower built the award, which leaves the distance between the company's mid-table sales and its outsized payout as the clearest feature of Mitra's year. Within this group, a company's sales offer a poor guide to the size of its leader's award.

Kasra Nejatian, chief executive of Opendoor Technologies, collected a $741.1 million package in 2025, and long-term stock awards made up the whole of it. No salary, cash bonus, or perk appears in that total. Every dollar of his reward rises or falls with Opendoor's share price, the same asset his investors hold.

Cash carried more weight for the typical executive on the list. Median cash compensation across the 100 CEOs, covering salaries, bonuses, and perks, edged up 3.6% to $6.1 million in 2025. Median stock awards climbed 44.6% to $28.4 million over the same stretch. Nejatian took none of the first category and a sum that dwarfs the second. His package pushes the list-wide tilt toward equity as far as it can go.

Opendoor's investors earned a 264.4% total shareholder return, the highest of any company in the study. The median company returned 12%. The study ties these results to Nejatian's pay, describing his equity-heavy structure as a match for Opendoor's strong market results. Owners and their chief executive profited from the same rally.

Opendoor's size does little to explain the award. The company booked $4,371 million in fiscal 2025 revenue, about $4.4 billion and below the $22.8 billion median for the 100 companies. Figma, at $1,055 million, is the lone company among the 10 largest packages with a smaller top line. Modest revenue beside an outsized award suggests that stock performance outweighed scale in setting Nejatian's payday.

The table lists no year-over-year change in pay for Nejatian. His compensation offers no growth story to measure. The case for his award rests on a single year of results. An all-stock package paired with the list's best shareholder return leaves his fortunes and his investors' moving in lockstep. If Opendoor's shares stumble, the same structure that produced this windfall would shrink his reward alongside theirs.

Robert J. Scaringe, chief executive of Rivian $RIVN Automotive, collected $402,639,080 for 2025, a 2,604% jump from the year before. Eight executives in this group of 10 show a reported change in pay, and the leaders of Wayfair, Broadcom $AVGO, and Welltower alone posted steeper climbs than his. Elon Musk and Kasra Nejatian carry no comparison figure. Scaringe's climb outpaced the increases at Figma, Goldman Sachs $GS, Warner Bros. Discovery, and Palo Alto Networks $PANW. Dylan Field's package at Figma is larger, a sign that the pace of a raise and the size of a paycheck tell separate stories. An increase of 2,604% means his 2025 award dwarfed whatever he received a year earlier. Scaringe sits in the upper half of this group on both total compensation and growth.

Rivian's $5.4 billion in fiscal 2025 revenue places it eighth among these 10 companies, above Figma and Opendoor. The automaker trails the $22.8 billion median revenue across the full roster of 100 firms. Scaringe's award exceeds what five businesses with heavier sales handed their leaders: Wayfair, Broadcom, Warner Bros. Discovery, Goldman Sachs, and Palo Alto Networks.

Tesla $TSLA, the table's other carmaker, outsells Rivian by a vast margin, and its chief collected the largest award of all. Welltower books heavier sales than Rivian and paid Shankh S. Mitra more than Scaringe. Tesla and Welltower fit the intuition that bigger companies hand their leaders bigger checks. The five other heavier sellers break that pattern, and Broadcom, Goldman Sachs, and Warner Bros. Discovery each outsell Rivian many times over.

Scale and generosity point in opposite directions at the low end of the revenue column. Figma, Opendoor, and Rivian, the three smallest businesses in the table by sales, handed their chiefs the second-, fourth-, and fifth-largest packages among the 10. Across this group, a company's revenue says little about what its leader earns, and outside Tesla, several of the richest awards gather where sales run lightest.

Niraj Shah collected $280,847,068 from Wayfair in 2025, a 99,088% increase and the largest change in pay anywhere in the table. Shah serves as chief executive, co-chairman, and co-founder of Wayfair Inc.

A gain of this size dwarfs the four-figure jumps that Shankh S. Mitra, Robert J. Scaringe, and Hock E. Tan posted. Those three increases already sit among the steepest in the group, yet none comes close to the scale of Shah's.

Shah's percentage reveals as much about his starting point as about his reward. A raise this large requires a modest base, so his prior-year pay must have amounted to a sliver of the 2025 total. The table does not disclose what he earned the year before or what form the new award took. Whatever the 2025 package contained, it marked a sharp break from what Shah had received before.

Wayfair booked $12.5 billion in fiscal 2025 revenue, the fifth-largest figure among the 10 companies. It outsold Figma, Welltower, Opendoor, Rivian $RIVN, and Palo Alto Networks $PANW. Tesla $TSLA, Broadcom $AVGO, Goldman Sachs $GS, and Warner Bros. Discovery each brought in more. Across all 100 companies in the study, median revenue reached $22.8 billion, a mark Wayfair's sales failed to clear. By that measure, the company sits in the middle of this group and below the midpoint of the full roster.

Shah's haul outstrips the sums granted to Tan of Broadcom, David M. Zaslav of Warner Bros. Discovery, and David M. Solomon of Goldman Sachs. Each of those three companies generated billions of dollars more in annual sales than Wayfair. The mismatch runs the other way, too. Welltower, Rivian, and Opendoor all brought in less than Wayfair, yet Mitra, Scaringe, and Kasra Nejatian each walked away with more than Shah. Among these 10 executives, a company's top line offers a poor guide to the size of its leader's paycheck.

Hock E. Tan collected $205,278,006 as Broadcom $AVGO's chief executive in 2025, a 7,692% increase over his prior-year pay. Of the eight executives in the table with a reported change in pay, only Niraj Shah of Wayfair posted a steeper climb. Tan's raise outpaced the gains of Shankh S. Mitra at Welltower and Robert J. Scaringe at Rivian $RIVN, two leaders who still took home bigger packages than he did. A jump of that magnitude shows how small his previous award was by comparison.

Broadcom brought in $63.9 billion in fiscal 2025 revenue, more than any company in this group except Tesla $TSLA. Its sales tower over the $22.8 billion median for the 100 companies in the study. The company's total still trails UnitedHealth $UNH Group, whose $447.6 billion topped the entire roster. Tan's package sits seventh in the table, well below the second-place standing Broadcom holds on revenue.

Pay and corporate scale part ways near the head of the table. Apart from Elon R. Musk, every executive who out-earned Tan runs a smaller business. Figma, Welltower, Opendoor, Rivian, and Wayfair each reported between $1.1 billion and $12.5 billion in sales. Wayfair, the largest of the five by revenue, sits a long way behind Broadcom. Company size cannot explain those five packages. Each of those leaders out-earned Tan while overseeing a smaller revenue base.

David M. Solomon of Goldman Sachs $GS and David M. Zaslav of Warner Bros. Discovery both earned less than Tan, and both of their companies generated less revenue than Broadcom. Nikesh Arora of Palo Alto Networks $PANW follows the same pattern, with a smaller paycheck and a smaller company. All three also received raises far slimmer than the one Tan collected. Among these 10 CEOs, Tan's award lines up with company size in a way the packages of Dylan Field, Mitra, Kasra Nejatian, Scaringe, and Shah do not.

David M. Zaslav of Warner Bros. Discovery collected $165,009,366 in 2025, a 218% increase over his prior-year pay.

Zaslav's raise looks restrained beside the surges around him. Shankh S. Mitra, Robert J. Scaringe, Hock E. Tan, and Niraj Shah each logged increases that ran to four or five digits in percentage terms. Dylan Field's 799% climb and David M. Solomon's 280% gain also outstripped Zaslav's. Among the eight executives in this group with a reported change in pay, only Nikesh Arora posted a smaller one.

A raise in the hundreds of percent delivered Zaslav a nine-figure sum. Only Solomon and Arora collected less, making the Warner Bros. Discovery award the third-smallest of the 10.

Warner Bros. Discovery generated $37.3 billion in fiscal 2025 revenue, the fourth-largest total among these 10 companies after Tesla $TSLA, Broadcom $AVGO, and Goldman Sachs $GS. Zaslav's company brought in more than Figma, Welltower, Opendoor, Rivian $RIVN, and Wayfair, yet the chiefs of all five firms received bigger awards than Zaslav. Goldman Sachs topped Warner Bros. Discovery in sales, yet Solomon took home less than Zaslav.

Revenue scale says little about the pace of raises, either. Among the four companies with the largest sales in this table, Warner Bros. Discovery and Goldman Sachs paired their totals with triple-digit gains for their chiefs, while Tan logged a four-digit jump at Broadcom. Musk's entry lists no change in pay, which leaves Tesla out of that comparison.

The Warner Bros. Discovery total also cleared the $22.8 billion median revenue across the full list of 100 companies. Clearing that median places Warner Bros. Discovery in the upper half of the 100 companies by sales. UnitedHealth $UNH Group outsold every company on that list with $447.6 billion, and its chief executive does not appear among these 10.

Across these 10 packages, neither a company's sales nor its position by revenue offers a reliable guide to what its leader earns or how fast that pay grows.

David M. Solomon received $118.9 million in total compensation from The Goldman Sachs $GS Group in 2025, 280% more than his prior-year award. His increase dwarfed the growth in median pay across the full roster of 100 chief executives. Among the 10, no other haul cleared nine figures by a thinner margin.

Goldman Sachs generated $59.4 billion in fiscal 2025 revenue, the third-largest figure among the 10 companies in the table, behind Tesla $TSLA and Broadcom $AVGO. Eight executives in that group collected bigger packages than Solomon. Broadcom's sales edged past Goldman's, and Hock E. Tan's award outran Solomon's. Warner Bros. Discovery, fourth in revenue among the 10, paid David M. Zaslav more than Goldman paid Solomon. Dylan Field, whose Figma posted the smallest revenue in the table, earned several times Solomon's total. Kasra Nejatian of Opendoor and Robert J. Scaringe of Rivian $RIVN outearned him while running companies with a fraction of Goldman's sales. Revenue offers a poor guide to executive rewards among these 10.

Critics faulted Solomon's leadership of Goldman Sachs after a stretch in which the firm's investment banking activity declined and its consumer business lost money. The setbacks did not stop Goldman's board from granting him a richer payout for 2025.

Solomon's gain sits toward the modest end of the changes in this group. The table reports no change for Elon Musk or Nejatian, which leaves eight executives to compare. Of those eight, Zaslav and Nikesh Arora logged smaller increases than Solomon. Field, Shankh S. Mitra, Scaringe, and Tan posted larger jumps, and three of them climbed by thousands of percent. Niraj Shah's increase ran into the tens of thousands of percent.

Nikesh Arora's $99,736,336 package at Palo Alto Networks $PANW makes him the only executive in this group of 10 whose 2025 pay stopped short of nine figures. Each of the other nine crossed the $100 million mark, a sweep the study counts as one more record. Arora's award rounds to $99.7 million, a sliver beneath the threshold his nine peers cleared. A single executive kept the table from a perfect run of nine-figure deals, and he missed by a margin narrower than the distance between any two neighbors on the list.

Arora's pay rose 72% from the prior year, the smallest increase among the eight executives in this table with a reported change. The other seven gains all topped 200%, and several packages swelled many times over in a single year. Arora joined the 10 on a raise that did not double his compensation. By the standards of this roster, his raise looks restrained.

Palo Alto Networks booked $9,221 million in fiscal 2025 revenue, or about $9.2 billion. Six companies in the lineup brought in more, and three brought in less. Figma, Opendoor, and Rivian $RIVN all trailed Palo Alto Networks on sales, yet each of their chief executives received an award several times larger than Arora's. Broadcom $AVGO and Goldman Sachs $GS posted the second- and third-largest revenue totals in the table, while their leaders hold packages among the four smallest. Company size offers a weak guide to where an executive lands among these 10.

Jane Fraser of Citigroup $C earned $95.8 million, the most among the four women on the full list. Her total fell below Arora's, and no woman appears among these 10. Arora's haul marks the entry point for this cohort, and the best-paid woman in the study never reached it. At the top of the 2025 study, every nine-figure package went to a man, and so did the record those nine deals set.

Billy Evans Answers ‘You Can See Everything’ Burning Questions on Alleged Cocaine Use, Nathan Fielder Regrets and More

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Billy Evans, Elizabeth Holmes’ romantic partner and the villainous third wheel in Nathan Fielder’s “You Can See Everything,” has taken to social media to answer burning questions about the documentary.

In a post on X Monday night, Evans invited viewers to “AMA” (ask me anything), with the addendum: “Needs to be legally permissable [sic].”

Asked to pick one misconception about the film to dispel, Evans said, “That I somehow control Liz. Anyone who actually knows her would find that idea hilarious. She’s fiercely independent, strongwilled [sic], and my best friend. We are partners in every sense. We challenge each other, disagree, and face the world together. Her closest friends are also some of my closest friends. If I ever mistreated her, they’d have my head.” Related Stories 'Nobody Wants This' Season 3 Opens With 6.2 Million Views in Four Days What to Watch on Netflix in November 2026

And asked if he regrets agreeing to the documentary, Evans wrote, “Not at all.” In case there was any doubt as to whether Evans was familiar with Fielder’s work, he said before the documentary he had seen “almost all of it.” Still, Evans said he and Holmes did “0” preparation ahead of filming with Fielder.

Evans said they celebrated Fielder’s birthday during production, “around the table in Del Mar with my family.” He said Fielder missed the Time 100 dinner — the magazine named the comedian one of the 100 most influential people in 2023 — so they celebrated that too.

Culture critic and podcast host Hunter Harris asked Evans whether he thought Christopher Abbott — who plays Evans in reenactments opposite Amanda Seyfried as Elizabeth Holmes — was miscast. Evasively, Evans replied, “Who is that?”

One question that came up repeatedly was whether Evans was high on cocaine during the filming of the documentary, which had been speculated due to his frequent sniffing. “Never been a fan,” Evans said when asked directly about the drug. “Solo parenting 2 toddlers you are sick approx 75% of days. Allergies in Austin TX are no joke,” he wrote. In another post, he asked, “Please someone from Austin back me up here.”

When one X user asked about his “facial bloating and the going insane part,” Evans responded, ” I got fat. Raising 2 kids alone, best friend in prison, depression, building a business. I ate more than I should. I was fat. Less so now. I prioritized my kids over my health. I’ve learned that I can find the time to do small things for myself again. And that eating after 10pm isn’t one of them.”

In “You Can See Everything,” Evans explains to Fielder that he has been posting on Holmes’ social media accounts while the Theranos founder is incarcerated. Evans said he does not need to get Holmes’ approval for the posts because, essentially, he knows what she would say. Plus, he holds power of attorney for Holmes.

“We talk about everything, including X. Nothing happened behind her back,” Evans wrote underneath a question about his control of her social media. “The POA was simply the legal mechanism that allowed me to act on her behalf while she’s incarcerated. It was not a substitute for her consent.”

Evans also weighed in on a few pop culture topics. Replying to music critic Anthony Fantano’s question, “is to pimp a butterfly a 10/10?,” Evans wrote, “Kendrick [Lamar] is a generational talent.” Asked about his “favorite pop diva,” Evans wrote, “Chappell Roan has one of the best live sets I have ever seen. The crowd was amazing. I bought our engagement ring at a Taylor Swift reputation tour merch stand. Aretha Franklin has the best voice.”

His four favorite films are “The Fall,” “Alien,” “The Great Dictator” and “Primer.” And when asked about popular podcaster Ethan Klein being accused of cheating on his wife, Hila Klein, Evans said, “The moral superiority of EK is absurd. His wife had every right to shame him publicly.”

On a sincere note, Evans replied to a few questions about Holmes. He said the first thing he will do when she gets out of prison is “hold our kids tight and talk all night.” And he wrote, “I miss everything about her. I miss her presence. Her touch. The way she runs her fingers through my hair. Her patience. She makes me a better person. And without her, I feel an emptiness that’s hard to describe. People often say they need someone when what they really mean is that they want them. I’ve thought a lot about that distinction. I need Liz. She should qualify as a medically necessary prescription.”

What does he not miss about his partner? “Def not this,” he replied to someone suggesting “her scrambled eggs.”

MLB rumors: How Yankees could make a Tarik Skubal deal work, plus Mets' priority and a Rangers pivot

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

The 2026-27 MLB offseason is less than one week old and already the hot stove moves and rumors are coming in. We've ranked the top 50 free agents and top 25 trade candidates, and built you a calendar of the important offseason dates and deadlines you need to know. Here now are Tuesday's hot stove happenings.

The Yankees are at least considering a run at Tarik Skubal, by far the best available free agent, and then trading another starting pitcher to address needs elsewhere on the roster, reports ESPN. New York is one of the few teams in baseball that legitimately does not need to add to its rotation this offseason, though there is no such thing as too much pitching.

This is the club's rotation depth chart at the moment:

Schlittler and the well-paid veterans aren't going anywhere. Warren is the most sensible trade chip. He threw 156 innings with a 3.92 ERA and solid under-the-hood indicators this past season and has four years of team control remaining. Cheap, controllable pitching is the game's greatest currency. It's always in demand and it can get you anything you need in a trade.

The Yankees need help at second and third base this offseason, possibly in center field and at catcher as well. Signing Skubal (or a different veteran) and trading Warren for help on the position player side is a sensible strategy for New York.

The Mets are focused on adding to their rotation and are "active everywhere," according to The Athletic. They're looking at the top of the market (i.e. Tarik Skubal) as well as at the small army of solid veterans who occupy the middle tier of the free agent class. The Mets could also explore the trade market, though the team is said to not be keen on dealing prospects this offseason.

At the moment, Nolan McLean and Christian Scott are the only locks for New York's 2027 rotation. Here is the team's starting pitching depth chart:

Senga could return to the bullpen after pitching admirably in the closer's role late this year. Clearly, the Mets need at least one starter this offseason and could really use more than one, just to lessen the reliance on young pitchers like Thornton and Tong. President of baseball operations David Stearns is disinclined to give pitchers long-term deals, which could put the Mets at a disadvantage for Skubal.

Gold Glove finalist Evan Carter underwent right hip surgery last week and will miss the start of the 2027 season, Rangers president of baseball operations Chris Young announced Monday. As a result, Texas will prioritize center field help this offseason. If needed, the Rangers can put left fielder Wyatt Langford (also a Gold Glove finalist) in center, but Langford is best in left.

"There's no doubt that we have to be cognizant that Evan's coming off a major surgery, and that we're going to have to find potential backfills if he is not ready to start the season, or if there are any setbacks," Young said, via MLB.com. "It doesn't change our belief in Evan long term, but it does change our short-term plans that we need to have a backfill and somebody who can certainly fill in or play a very solid center field and hopefully contribute offensively."

The free agent class does not offer much in center field unless you're willing to take on injury or age-related decline risk. Here are the top available free agent center fielders according to FanGraphs projected 2027 WAR:

Jarren Duran (Red Sox) and Steven Kwan (Guardians) stand out as center field trade candidates, though Kwan in particular will come at a high price. A cheaper, defense-first stopgap like, say, Denzel Clarke (Athletics) or Blake Perkins (Guardians) may be the best short-term option while Carter is on the mend.

Nearly 1 In 3 Hyundais Sold In October Was A Hybrid As High Gas Prices Push Buyers To Pivot

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

A whopping 29% of Hyundai’s total October sales volume in the U.S. were hybrids, the automaker said Tuesday. That’s the highest mix ever for gas-electric powertrains for the Korean automaker, thanks to buyers seeking more fuel efficient cars due to gas prices that have remained stubbornly high since the start of the U.S. war in Iran.

Hyundai doesn't break out hybrid sales in its monthly reports, instead bundling them together under individual model names. But it did say that hybrid versions of the Santa Fe and Palisade set new October sales records. Meanwhile, sales of the all-electric Ioniq 5 bounced back, climbing 21% year over year to 1,981 units.

Hyundai sold 73,452 vehicles in October, a 5% increase over the same month last year. Its year-to-date sales now stand at 770,916 units, up 3% from 2025.

Sister brand Kia also reported record overall and electrified vehicle sales. It sold 80,008 vehicles last month, bringing its year-to-date total to 747,394 units, not far behind Hyundai.

Kia's hybrid sales jumped 114% year over year, helped by strong demand for hybrid versions of the popular Sportage and Sorento SUVs and the Carnival minivan. But the more interesting news is the recovery in Kia's EV sales, which have struggled for several quarters.

Sales of the three-row EV9 surged 111% to 1,406 units, while EV6 sales climbed 58% to 803 units. Kia also sold 976 units of the more affordable EV3, whose production and deliveries are still ramping up following its recent U.S. launch.

There's a caveat to those EV growth figures, though. The Ioniq 5, EV6, and EV9 were all coming off a particularly weak October 2025, when sales cratered following the expiration of the federal EV tax credit a month earlier.

The months leading up to the September 2025 deadline saw buyers rush to claim the incentive, pulling demand forward and causing subsequent sales to decline. The broader EV market has been gradually recovering since, with the third quarter marking its strongest performance since then.

The EV3, meanwhile, could give Kia's electric lineup another shot in the arm. Starting at $31,385 including destination, it's among the cheapest EVs on sale in the U.S., alongside the Nissan Leaf and Chevrolet Bolt. We recently reviewed the small electric crossover and think it offers really good value.

Hyundai could also see another boost to its EV business in the coming months. The automaker is preparing to supply Waymo with tens of thousands of Ioniq 5-based robotaxis, Hyundai CEO José Muñoz said in September. That could be a new source of demand for its electric crossover, beyond individual consumers.

Early Week 9 NFL betting, odds: 3 wagers that I like right now

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As we enter Week 9, it's a good time to remind ourselves that this is only the midway point of the NFL regular season.

Subjectively, it feels like we are deep into the NFL calendar, and my opinions on a lot of these teams are fully formed. That is not true in reality. Some teams will completely surprise us in the second half of the season, and others we feel we can trust will fall off.

Last season, the Indianapolis Colts were 7-1, had the best offense in the league and ended the season with a losing record. The Texans, who started out 0-3 last year, finished on a nine-game winning streak and were considered a very dangerous team in the playoffs.

My candidates for a very strong second half of the season include the 3-5 Dallas Cowboys and 3-5 Tampa Bay Buccaneers. My candidates for regression include the 6-1 Minnesota Vikings and the 4-2-1 New York Giants. If you are looking for a midseason future, start there with their win totals and odds to make or miss the playoffs.

Here are three early bets I like in Week 9.

The first reason to target this game is the looming NFL trade deadline. The Colts have announced a fire sale after firing their general manager and already trading guard Quenton Nelson to the Seattle Seahawks and DT DeForest Buckner to the San Francisco 49ers. Next up are likely WR Josh Downs (requested a trade), RB Jonathan Taylor (targeted by multiple teams) and DT Grover Stewart. If just one of these pieces were to be moved, the spread should move onto or through the key number of 7.

On the field, the Cowboys average the seventh-most points in the NFL (27.4 points per game) and should easily be able to move the ball against a porous Indianapolis defense that allows the third-most points (27.8 PPG). The passing game is the main point of vulnerability for the Colts defense, and Dak Prescott will exploit that with high volume to his star WR duo in CeeDee Lamb and George Pickens.

Inversely, Dallas' defense has struggled to stop opposing teams, but the Colts' offense is anemic. Daniel Jones has the second-most interceptions (nine), and the Colts have a league worst -11 net turnover differential due to six lost fumbles. The Cowboys defense doesn't have to consistently make plays, it needs to make one or two possession-changing plays to completely alter the game.

The main story out of Baltimore this week is a potentially season-ending injury for QB Lamar Jackson. Jackson returned from a high-ankle sprain on Sunday, but was limited to literally one snap where his hand hit an opposing defender's helmet following through on a throw, and his thumb was notably dislocated and deformed. Jackson now heads to surgery, is placed on IR and it is being reported to be season ending. Backup QB Tyler "Snoop" Huntley is also dealing with turf toe and is unlikely to play as well on a short week Thursday Night Football game, leaving Joe Fagnano to likely be this week's starter.

My thoughts on betting this game are twofold. First, it is a prime candidate to put the Jaguars in a six-point teaser and bring the -8.5 down to a -2.5 favorite and pair with another leg that moves through both 3 and 7. Other candidates to pair the Jaguars with include the Cleveland Browns, Green Bay Packers and Carolina Panthers who are all +2.5 in market (to be brought up to +8.5). My favorite of the bunch would be the Browns.

The second is to attack the derivative markets in props and team totals for edges. The best chance for the Ravens to win means limiting the amount of possessions and not getting into a track meet with a high-powered offense. That likely means Derrick Henry going over on his 17.5 rush attempts is a strong prop angle, or the under on the Jaguars team total.

Because of the quarterback situation, I suspect Henry takes a few direct snap rushing attempts in a wildcat formation, and thus the rushing attempts are the best place to go. Jaguars WR Parker Washington to go under 4.5 receptions was also a candidate for me.

Bet: Derrick Henry over 17.5 rush attempts (-130)

The two main storylines from these teams' respective Week 8 games were zero completions for Baker Mayfield, and a staunch defensive performance from newcomer Jeffery Simmons and the Bears defense. Praising one team's defense and talking down upon the opposing offense would lead most bettors to look towards the under. I'm not trying to be contrarian, but I see things differently here.

The Buccaneers were playing in a massive rain storm where the quarterbacks could not grip the ball — that negates pretty much all context from that game. What I am most looking towards is the health improvement of Mayfield's throwing thumb and WR Emeka Egbuka. Mayfield injured his throwing thumb in Week 3 and missed three games after that in what was reported as a three-to-seven week injury.

Mayfield came back at the earliest possible time relative to his injury. His ability to grip and rip the football should only be improving, considering his grip is exactly what kept him out for that period of time. Egbuka is still unknown, but getting a young WR1 can only boost the offense. He cleared concussion protocol but is still dealing with a foot injury.

On the other side of the ball, we are all at a loss for words regarding this Bears offense. On Monday Night against Seattle, they boasted advanced metrics of 51% success rate and a +0.2 EPA per play. For reference, that is the best performance from any team all year going against the Seattle Seahawks defense. While Tampa Bay is strong defensively, it seems nothing can stop the Bears from moving the ball downfield.

Realty Income Has Paid 676 Consecutive Monthly Dividends. Here’s How Its Earnings Results Fit the Bigger Picture.

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Realty Income’s latest results show the cash flow behind its payout and how modest its growth really is.

There's no question that Realty Income (O -0.71%) is my favorite dividend stock. I appreciate the consistency and convenience of a monthly dividend, and I love the company's commitment to dividend growth.

And now that Realty Income reported its third-quarter earnings report on Nov. 2, I see no reason to change my mind.

The real estate investment trust recently declared its 676th consecutive monthly dividend, a track record that stretches back more than 56 years. But the real story isn't how long the company has been paying dividends. It's how Realty Income continues to generate the cash needed to keep those payments coming.

Its third-quarter earnings report offers some encouraging evidence.

Realty Income's third-quarter revenue was $1.62 billion, up 10.3% from a year ago. Net income was $403.9 million and $0.43 per share versus $315.8 million and $0.35 per share last year.

But for mortgage REIT investors, adjusted funds from operations (AFFO) may be even more important. This metric adjusts earnings for items such as real estate depreciation, providing a better picture of the cash-generating performance that supports dividends.

Realty Income's AFFO was up 3.7% year over year to $1.12 per share, up 4 cents a share from a year ago. That might not sound particularly exciting, especially when compared with the growth rates of high-flying technology stocks. But Realty Income isn't supposed to be exciting. It's supposed to produce dependable income, and that's exactly what its business continues to do.

Management raised its AFFO guidance for the full year to a range of $4.45 and $4.46 per share, while reaffirming its $10 billion investment target for 2026.

Realty Income's business model is fairly straightforward. The company owns about 15,500 commercial properties and leases them to tenants, typically under long-term net leases that require tenants to cover many property-related expenses. It has clients in every U.S. state, the U.K., and eight other European countries.

Its diversified client base spans nearly 100 industries, including grocery stores, convenience stores, home improvement retailers, pharmacies, and other businesses that serve everyday consumer needs. Dollar General, its largest tenant, accounts for just 3.3% of annualized contractual rent, while 7-Eleven accounts for another 3%.

Realty Income has also demonstrated remarkable consistency in maintaining occupancy across its properties. Its year-end occupancy rate hasn't fallen below 96% since 1992, including during the financial crisis and the COVID-19 pandemic.

Meanwhile, the company continues to expand. Realty Income invested $2.6 billion during the third quarter, with its proportionate share totaling $2.2 billion. Those investments had an initial weighted-average cash yield of 6.8%.

The company also expanded its private capital business into Europe through a joint venture with KKR. These arrangements can provide an additional source of capital for investments without relying exclusively on Realty Income's balance sheet.

The appeal of Realty Income has never been the prospect of explosive share-price appreciation. Investors can find plenty of other companies offering faster growth.

What makes Realty Income special is the combination of recurring rental income, a diversified property portfolio, and a long history of rewarding shareholders.

During the third quarter, the company paid $0.813 per share in dividends, compared with $1.12 in AFFO. That's a payout ratio of roughly 73%, leaving room to fund other needs while continuing to reward shareholders.

And that's why the latest earnings report fits so neatly into the bigger picture.

Realty Income doesn't need spectacular earnings growth to deliver on its investment proposition. It needs dependable tenants, disciplined acquisitions, and enough cash flow to keep increasing its dividend over time.

After 676 consecutive monthly dividends, that approach has a pretty convincing track record. And it's why Realty Income remains my favorite dividend stock.

Patrick Sanders has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Dollar General, KKR, and Realty Income. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

THE INSIGHT Harri utilises a network of officiating and industry contacts to deliver verified, expert analysis of refereeing decisions and PGMOL policy. He provides the regulatory clarity behind the controversy to ensure fans get the full picture.

Newcastle United star Lewis Hall is hardly one to get caught up in contentious incidents, but that changed against Everton.

The left-back is always far too focused on his football to get involved. Emotions were flying high at St James' Park on Monday, 2 November, though.

Newcastle's clash with the Toffees ultimately ended in disappointment, with Thierno Barry scoring for the visitors just four minutes into the second half.

That was compounded when Jacob Ramsey received two yellow cards in quick succession, leading to a red card after clattering Harrison Armstrong.

Replays showed that the midfielder was caught extremely high on the leg by his opponent, and it probably should have been a straight red card.

And this is where Hall inexplicably gets involved.

The 22-year-old could be seen trying to drag Armstrong back to his feet, despite his teammate's tackle potentially causing serious injury.

While Brennan Johnson and Fabian Schar both had their names taken, Hall somehow escaped a yellow card, and it should have been even harsher.

Fans across the Premier League were apoplectic when Gabriel Martinelli attempted to throw Conor Bradley off the pitch.

Liverpool's full-back is still sidelined with that injury, by the way. For all Hall knew, Harrison was seriously injured and he could have made it worse.

This type of thing needs to be stamped out of football. It's up to the referee to decide how to proceed, not the opposition full-back.

Hall wouldn't be the only player missing, with Ramsey now sidelined for one game after receiving his marching orders against the Toffees.

Keith Hackett told Football Insider that the midfielder should be forced to serve a three-game ban, though, due to just how bad the challenge was.

It was high, dangerous, and Ramsey was just totally out of control. It was still a sending-off, but two yellow cards doesn't seem to do it justice.

Lionsgate is teaming up with Sony for Destin Daniel Cretton's anime adaptation

The live-action Naruto movie is officially moving forward, thanks to some help from Sony. The anime adaptation from Destin Daniel Cretton (Spider-Man: Brand New Day, Short Term 12) has been in the works at Lionsgate and is now set to begin production next year. This marks yet another collaboration between Cretton and Sony, a partnership that saw massive success this year with the latest Tom Holland Spider-Man movie.

In an official press release, Lionsgate announced that Sony Pictures will co-finance and distribute Naruto internationally following a highly competitive bidding war. With Sony onboard, Lionsgate has now greenlit the project and scheduled production to start in spring 2027. Lionsgate will oversee the movie's development, production, and domestic distribution.

Destin Daniel Cretton will write, direct, and produce. On reuniting with Cretton for Naruto, Sony Pictures CEO Tom Rothman said that "we had a sweet success this summer with Destin and are delighted to be back in his capable hands," also calling Lionsgate's project an A-level adaptation. Following the success of Brand New Day this year, Sony entered a three-year deal in which Cretton will develop and produce films for the company under Hisako, the production company he runs with Jeyun Munford.

The live-action film will be based on the series by Masashi Kishimoto, which has become a global sensation across manga, anime, and video games. Naruto follows the titular character, who starts the story as a young orphan in a fantasy world inspired by Japan's feudal era. Naruto dreams of becoming Hokage, essentially the village leader, and pursues that dream alongside an ever-expanding supporting cast.

This July, Cretton announced that a global casting search for the film had begun to find the series' main trio (called Team 7): Naruto, Sasuke, and Sakura. While Lionsgate and Cretton haven't confirmed any casting yet, it has also been reported that Charles Melton (Beef) is in talks for the role of Kakashi, Team 7's mentor.

Soviet Nostalgia Is a Geopolitical Dead End

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: Soviet Nostalgia Is a Geopolitical Dead End

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Understanding the conflict four years on.

Across the former Soviet Union and its satellite states, almost everyone knows The Irony of Fate, a 1976 romantic comedy in which a Moscow surgeon gets drunk at a bathhouse on New Year’s Eve and ends up on a plane to Leningrad. He gives a taxi driver his Moscow address, “Third Builder’s Street,” and arrives at an identical block of flats, where his key opens the door.

The world that the movie describes was once much larger than Russia itself. You could arrive in Kyiv, Tbilisi, or Riga and speak Russian; quote the same Soviet films; laugh at the same jokes; and assume that underneath all the flags, languages, and newly drawn borders there was still some common social and cultural space holding the place together.

Across the former Soviet Union and its satellite states, almost everyone knows The Irony of Fate, a 1976 romantic comedy in which a Moscow surgeon gets drunk at a bathhouse on New Year’s Eve and ends up on a plane to Leningrad. He gives a taxi driver his Moscow address, “Third Builder’s Street,” and arrives at an identical block of flats, where his key opens the door.

The world that the movie describes was once much larger than Russia itself. You could arrive in Kyiv, Tbilisi, or Riga and speak Russian; quote the same Soviet films; laugh at the same jokes; and assume that underneath all the flags, languages, and newly drawn borders there was still some common social and cultural space holding the place together.

When Russia set out to restore that world by force, some of its nostalgic talking points, once confined to state television, found a wide company in the West. Tucker Carlson, who has turned it into a routine, tells his audience that Ukraine is weak, corrupt, and finished. Candace Owens flew to Russia and came back saying, “they are lying to us about Russia.” Others, such as U.S. Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene, refuse to support aid for a country defending itself from invasion.

John Mearsheimer and Noam Chomsky have built long careers explaining that whatever Russia does is somebody else’s fault. Francesca Albanese, a U.N. special rapporteur, has called for an end to arms deliveries to a country fighting against an invader.

The left and the right rarely agree on anything, yet here, some of their thinkers converge: Sooner or later, Kyiv must learn to live with Moscow’s demands. Whether it’s Owen Matthews writing in the Spectator that Ukraine is losing the war or Jeffrey Sachs insisting that Kyiv will never join NATO, the underlying belief is the same. For some people shaped by that world, its disappearance is sad. There is a little of Anton Chekhov’s The Cherry Orchard in them: They are so attached to the estate that they can scarcely imagine a world beyond it.

Leaving Russia for the West did not always mean leaving its imperial assumptions behind. Joseph Brodsky, persecuted and exiled by the Soviet state, greeted Ukrainian independence with a poem full of insults, imagining Ukrainians returning to Russian poet Alexander Pushkin on their deathbeds rather than their own Taras Shevchenko. Pushkin himself, in “To the Slanderers of Russia,” told Europe to stay out of what he presented as a family quarrel among Slavs during Russia’s suppression of the Polish uprising in 1831.

What is striking is how many people who never lived in that world have arrived at the same conclusion. These pundits identify Ukraine’s problems well. Kyiv is short of men, money, and Patriot interceptors; corruption persists; and its allies’ declarations have far outrun their policies. They are right about the diagnosis and wrong about the cure.

They also exaggerate the threat. Russia has advanced at enormous cost to Ukraine, yet a snail set off from its starting positions might have reached parts of today’s front line first. Ukraine won the decisive battle for Kyiv, when Moscow’s aim was a disarmed Ukraine under a government of its choosing. It need not recover all its territory to win. If it remains sovereign, secure, and in the West, then Russia’s larger project will have failed. The only real Russian victory is the one that these arguments point toward—a Ukraine that is left vulnerable and subject to Moscow’s will.

The worst part of this so-called realist take is the claim that it is for Ukraine’s own good. By the forecasts of these observers, Ukraine was supposed to lose in 2022, then every year since. Instead of revising the premise, they move the date forward and wait for history to rescue the thinking.

Even the more serious skeptics concede that Russian President Vladimir Putin has not changed his war aims and that U.S. special envoy Jared Kushner is naive to think that business deals will seduce the Kremlin into peace. If Putin cannot be persuaded, what is Ukraine supposed to negotiate? A concession to a man who demands the maximum will only be a down payment. Georgia learned this the hard way. Its 2008 cease-fire with Russia was followed by the latter’s invasion of Crimea; the Minsk agreements were followed by the full-scale invasion.

Another argument that keeps resurfacing is that the West provoked the war. Mearsheimer and Chomsky describe Russian aggression as a response to a menacing NATO, the supposed existential threat that Putin cites and his Western interpreters obligingly repeat. Yet Moscow was already using force in Moldova and Georgia in the early 1990s, when NATO enlargement was hardly an available excuse. You can call those ethnic conflicts, as some do, but after so many Russian acts of aggression, ignoring the reality takes real determination. The only power that has invaded anyone in the neighborhood is Russia. NATO only prevents it from doing so.

What runs through all this is an old fatalism, very familiar in Russia, in which Russian power is a constant, Western resolve is fickle, and Ukrainian resistance is doomed. It calls itself realism, but it is really pessimism, in which history can change everything except Russia’s behavior. It treats Moscow’s desires as objective reality and Ukraine’s goals as childish aspirations, in the spirit of the Brezhnev Doctrine, where small countries choose freely, provided that Moscow approves.

Soviet power once looked equally permanent; in Alexei Yurchak’s words, “everything was forever, until it was no more.” The Baltic states were told that Russia would never tolerate their joining NATO. They did not move on the map. The policy and debate did, because Western governments decided it should. Yes, they joined while Russia was weak. That is exactly the point.

If Western policy has failed, then the policy should change. If Ukraine lacks air defense, the answer is more air defense. Pressure on Russia should grow until Moscow understands that the West will hold its ground and that the war will end as a strategic loss even if Russia takes the rest of the Donbas, which it does not really need. That means guaranteeing Ukraine’s future security and denying Moscow that lever. Is there a Western consensus on this yet? No. Let’s hope one arrives, because otherwise the problems will end up far closer to home.

Why, then, does the argument keep returning? For some, it is a hatred of the West so complete that every war turns into a story about American wrongdoing. For others, it is audience. Outrage and fringe arguments sell, and Moscow makes a good backdrop for a story about Western decline. And for more than a few, it is simply clear sympathy for Russia, for its strongman, its bigotry framed as tradition, its fantasy of itself as the third Rome against a decadent West.

Then for others, it is fear. In my village in the mountains of Georgia, my neighbor, a man called Valeri, says much the same thing as these commentators, though one should be more forgiving of Valeri. Russian troops already occupy part of his country, and one morning, he may discover that his barn is on the Russian side of the line. When he says that Russia is too big and too dangerous, one can understand. His is a foreign policy of the village bench. But the same worldview, minus the cows, turns up in American studios and newspapers, among people who know that they have more choices.

Whether they echo that view from Moscow or in the West, they will find the Ukraine that comes out of this war even harder to recognize. Young Ukrainians speak more Ukrainian, consume less Russian culture, and understand fewer of the Soviet references that once traveled from Moscow to Mykolaiv. After generations of Russification, the recovery of a national consciousness is not an easy task. It may come with mistakes. But a country bombed in the name of a supposedly common history, that took tyranny and bloodshed to hold together, cannot be expected to preserve that history for the comfort of people who remember it fondly.

Putin can drop more bombs and probably will, but after nearly five years of war, it is hard to imagine Ukrainians deciding that capitulation is the answer. Like other empires, the Russian one will sooner or later collapse, including in people’s heads. Along with it will go the longing to walk into Kyiv or Tbilisi and expect the keys to fit.

Ani Chkhikvadze is a Georgian reporter based in Washington.

Why Paris Jackson Is Seeking $600 Million From Michael Jackson Estate

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Paris Jackson is taking legal action against her father Michael Jackson’s estate, claiming she and brothers Prince Jackson and Bigi Jackson haven’t received the proper funds in their trust.

Paris Jackson is taking legal action against her father’s estate.

Seventeen years after Michael Jackson’s death, his daughter is asking his estate to release $600 million into a family trust for her and brothers Prince Jackson and Bigi Jackson, according to court documents reviewed by E! News.

In the Oct. 29 filing, Paris claims that while the estate has generated billions of dollars since her father’s 2009 passing, neither she nor her siblings have received its funding in the trust “Michael intended" to provide for them.

Hence, the 28-year-old is asking a Los Angeles judge to order a partial distribution of the estate’s assets to the Michael Jackson Family Trust, which was set up to take care of Michael’s three kids financially.

Under its terms, Paris as well as Prince, 29, and Bigi, 24, were supposed to begin receiving income from their respective shares when they turned 21 before receiving more portions at age 30 and additional distributions at 35 and 40, according to the filing.

However, the docs state that “Michael Jackson’s family trust remains unfunded.”

And according to Paris’ filing, the lack of funding has left the siblings dependent on the estate’s remaining executor John Branca for the money.

“They instead receive lesser amounts dictated by the remaining Executor,” the docs allege, “who uses this power to deter them from checking his abuses.”

However, The Michael Jackson Estate shared in a statement to People that it cannot fund the trusts in full until other legal matters are resolved, including ongoing litigation with the Internal Revenue Service, which sought $700 million in taxes and penalties.

“The Estate was able to secure a ruling to significantly reduce that amount, but the matter has not been settled and could still change,” the estate told the outlet before denying Paris’ claim that it’s been withholding or reducing payments to the beneficiaries, calling the accusation “a complete fabrication.”

The estate added that more than $200 million had already been distributed for their benefit and noted that, according to Michael’s will, “None of his children receive a distribution until they are in their 30s.”

The statement concluded, “The Estate looks forward to the time when all issues are resolved so that their trusts can be fully funded as Michael wanted.”

But a spokesperson for Paris disputed the estate’s response, telling the outlet, “The Estate is once again deflecting from its own incompetence. Paris called for the trust to be partially funded, something Mr. Branca had the ability and obligation to do long ago.”

The statement continued, “After years of delay, Paris had no choice but to step in herself.”

Trump Remains ‘Laser-Focused’ on Housing Affordability Despite Final Midterm Speech on DC Reflecting Pool, White House Says

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In his final message to voters ahead to the pivotal midterm elections, President Donald Trump touted renovations to the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool without uttering a word about housing affordability. Still, the White House says he remains squarely focused on the issue.

"President Trump has been laser-focused on making housing more affordable," White House spokesman Davis Ingle told Realtor.com® in a statement.

Ingle noted that Trump in his second term had signed an executive order prohibiting large Wall Street firms from purchasing single-family homes, directed Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac to purchase $200 billion in mortgage bonds that helped drive rates to four-year lows, and "cut unnecessary red tape at a historic pace to boost supply, speed construction, and lower costs."

"The President will not stop fighting until the American Dream of homeownership is within reach for every American, which is why he continues to sign bold new executive orders and calls on Congress to pass further legislation," he said.

During his speech on Monday evening by the reflecting pool in the heart of Washington, DC, Trump spoke at length and in exhaustive detail about the extensive repairs to leaky pipes, surface coating, and cracked granite.

"This is a massive piece of pool," Trump told an audience of several dozen people gathered for the unveiling of the refurbished monument, which has been one of his signature projects since the opening months of his second term.

Trump reiterated claims that the pool had been vandalized by "sick" saboteurs. However, federal prosecutors and the U.S. Attorney's Office for DC previously acknowledged in court filings that the damage to the pool lining that accompanied massive algae blooms was the product of shoddy installation by contractors rushing to complete the job before the 250th Independence Day festivities.

Beyond his breakdown of the pool makeover, which is projected to cost taxpayers about $16 million, Trump touted his record in his second White House term, claiming that his administration had reduced crime in DC by "close to 90%," carried out sweeping beautification projects, and cleared out homeless encampments.

It was not until 23 minutes into his address that Trump finally mentioned the looming midterm election, which will determine control of Congress and could shape the final two years of his time in the White House.

"This is the last day before the midterm elections, the most important midterm, I believe in our lifetime," said the president.

The stakes could hardly be higher: control of the U.S. Senate and House, dozens of governorships, crucial ballot measures on housing, and the legislative direction of the second half of Trump's term.

Final preelection polling showed Democrats holding a meaningful advantage in the generic congressional ballot and leading in several key Senate battlegrounds, including Michigan, Texas, and Maine, driven by widespread voter frustration with the surging costs of fuel, groceries, and housing.

While Trump did not bring up affordability in his remarks, Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum, who joined him at the event in DC, briefly touched upon the subject, arguing that Trump's policies have paved the way to "food security, more grazing for our country, more timber production for affordable homes, and more oil and gas to drive the price and affordability of energy down in our country."

This fall, average 30-year mortgage rates surged back past the 7% mark, propelled by surging oil prices linked to the president's ongoing war in Iran, severely eroding homebuyers' purchasing power.

The broader cost-of-living squeeze has not gone unnoticed by the electorate across the political spectrum, prompting Trump to hit the campaign trail in traditional Republican strongholds like Texas and South Carolina to shore up support for vulnerable GOP candidates.

Yet in his concluding remarks on the eve of the election, the president struck an uncharacteristically fatalistic tone and appeared resigned to a potential "blue wave."

"Usually whoever the sitting president is—Republican or Democrat, good or bad, good president, great president, bad—for some reason, the sitting president doesn’t win the midterms," he said. "But we gave this a very strong shot."

Snejana Farberov is a reporter at Realtor.com covering the U.S. housing market and the latest domestic real estate trends. She has worked as a general assignment journalist in New York City and Long Island for 16 years, writing for New York Post, Daily Mail, and News 12. Snejana earned bachelor's degrees in journalism and Italian from St. John's University, followed by a master’s degree from Columbia University School of Journalism.
